```text
1	# Idea Brief: Beyond Intent Drift: Persuasion-Grounded Turn Representations for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection
2	
3	
4	- **Idea ID**: `idea_008`
5	- **Created**: 2026-05-17
6	- **Approved**: 2026-05-17
7	- **Source**: foreign:be-idea-gen-0515-5
8	- **Level**: top-tier
9	- **Target Venues**: EMNLP 2026 (ARR submission deadline: May 25, 2026; commitment deadline: August 2, 2026; conference: October 24-29, Budapest) — LLM Security is listed as a topic area within the main conference, with additional relevance to Discourse and Pragmatics, NLP Applications, and Ethics/Bias/Fairness topic areas
10	
11	## Seed Topic
12	
13	Target: EMNLP 2026, ARR submission deadline 2026-05-25 (11 days from today 2026-05-14). Track: LLM Security.
14	
15	Direction: LLM safety robustness — defense methods against jailbreaking, adversarial prompt attacks, and safety alignment degradation. In-scope: robust safety training that resists adaptive attacks, automated red-teaming frameworks for systematic vulnerability discovery, safety-aware decoding or filtering at inference time, detecting and mitigating safety degradation when models are fine-tuned or distilled, multi-turn attack defense, tool-use and agentic safety. NOT limited to text — agentic tool-call safety and multi-modal attack surfaces welcome.
16	
17	Contribution type: METHOD ONLY. Must propose a concrete defense algorithm, detection pipeline, or training recipe — not just a new attack or red-teaming dataset. Attack + defense paired contributions OK if defense is the primary contribution.
18	
19	HARD CONSTRAINTS:
20	1. Compute: ≤200 H100-hours total. Safety research is mostly inference-heavy — budget for evaluation across multiple attack suites, not large-scale pretraining.
21	2. Time: ≤11 days. Build on existing open safety benchmarks and models (Llama-3-Guard, ShieldGemma, Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1 distills). Use established attack libraries (HarmBench, JailbreakBench, StrongREJECT) for evaluation.
22	3. Eval: Attack Success Rate (ASR) reduction on HarmBench, JailbreakBench, AdvBench; safety score on WildGuard/Aegis; utility preservation on MT-Bench/MMLU (defense must not kill helpfulness).
23	
24	This is a NEW dedicated track at EMNLP 2026 — high visibility, growing fast, few established defense methods. 2026 research shows reasoning models can jailbreak other models at 97% success; defense lags far behind attack. Look for: inference-time safety filters that adapt to attack patterns, representation-level safety mechanisms (circuit-based), robust alignment under fine-tuning distribution shift, defense against multi-turn and agentic attacks. Avoid: yet another jailbreak attack without defense contribution, prompt-template-only defenses, safety classifiers without adversarial robustness guarantees.
25	
26	## Core Problem
27	
28	Multi-turn jailbreak attacks remain one of the most persistent threats to deployed LLMs, distributing adversarial intent across individually benign-seeming messages that exploit conversational dynamics to gradually steer models past safety boundaries. The state-of-the-art dedicated multi-turn detector, DeepContext (arXiv 2602.16935, Feb 2026), models this threat as generic intent drift: it embeds each turn with fine-tuned BERT, applies task-attention weighted pooling, and feeds the resulting sequence through a 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden units) with hybrid residual connections to detect when the conversation trajectory shifts toward harmful territory. This approach achieves F1=0.84 on standard multi-turn benchmarks with sub-20ms inference on a T4 GPU, establishing a strong baseline. However, DeepContext's reliance on undifferentiated semantic drift creates three fundamental limitations. (1) High false-positive rates on naturally drifting conversations: brainstorming sessions, Socratic dialogues, creative writing, and complex tutoring all exhibit substantial topic drift, triggering drift detectors even though no manipulation is occurring. (2) Zero interpretability: the 2048-dimensional GRU hidden state provides an opaque risk score with no actionable threat intelligence—security teams cannot see which manipulation strategy is being employed or at which turn the attack transitions from rapport-building to exploitation. (3) The acknowledged 'glass cannon' effect—strong performance on known attack templates but fragile generalization to novel strategies—because the model learns surface-level correlates of attacks (lexical drift patterns) rather than the underlying causal mechanism (adversarial persuasion). A recent representation engineering analysis (Bullwinkel et al., arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025) provides a mechanistic explanation for why per-turn representation-level defenses fundamentally struggle with multi-turn attacks: Crescendo keeps model outputs in a 'benign' region of representation space, with harmful classification dropping sharply after just 2 turns. Circuit breakers—trained to reroute harmful single-turn representations—achieve only 54.2% ASR defense (vs. 3.8% on single-turn) because multi-turn attack representations are 'sufficiently different from single-turn representations.' This finding demonstrates that the information needed to detect multi-turn attacks is not reliably present in per-turn representations of semantic content; it resides instead in the discourse-level pattern of how turns relate to each other—specifically, in the adversarial persuasion playbook orchestrating the conversation. Meanwhile, BIID (arXiv 2509.22732) achieves near-0% ASR through bidirectional LLM-based intention inference, but imposes 2+ full LLM reasoning passes per turn with latency estimated at several seconds (no published latency analysis exists; this estimate is based on the computational requirements of two full LLM forward passes per turn and requires independent verification). GLiGuard (arXiv 2605.07982, May 2026) demonstrates that compact 300M-parameter encoders can detect 11 jailbreak strategies and 14 harm categories in a single pass with 16x speedup over 7B decoder guards, but operates only on single inputs without temporal modeling. XGuard-Train (arXiv 2504.13203) achieves 48.3% vulnerability reduction through safety training data (30K multi-turn conversations), but as a training-time defense, it is model-specific and inapplicable to closed-source or already-deployed models. The core problem is therefore not architectural—encoder-plus-recurrent designs are well-established for this task—but representational: the features extracted from each turn do not encode the information most relevant to distinguishing adversarial manipulation from benign conversational evolution, and as Bullwinkel et al. show, per-turn semantic representations are actively misleading because multi-turn attacks are designed to keep them benign. What turn-level representation would capture the causal mechanism of multi-turn attacks at the discourse level, above the representation space where attacks deliberately hide?
29	
30	## Opportunity / Why Now
31	
32	Four converging findings define a precise representational opportunity. First, Bullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025) established that multi-turn jailbreaks succeed specifically because they keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region of representation space, explaining why representation-level defenses (circuit breakers) fail with a 50-percentage-point generalization gap. This finding has a critical implication: effective multi-turn detection requires features that operate above the per-turn representation level—at the discourse level where the manipulation playbook is visible even when individual turns appear benign. Second, 'How Do LLMs Persuade?' (arXiv 2508.05625) demonstrated that linear probes applied to intermediate transformer representations can reliably detect persuasion strategies, track persuasion success trajectories, and identify personality-adapted persuasion across multi-turn conversations—faster than prompting-based approaches and with competitive accuracy. Although this work was not applied to jailbreak detection, it empirically validates a crucial premise: the specific persuasion mechanisms operating in a conversation are extractable from turn-level representations and computationally tractable. Third, Hagendorff (Nature Communications 2026) showed that reasoning-model attackers achieving 97% ASR employ identifiable persuasion strategies—flattery/rapport-building (84.75%), educational/research framing (68.56%), hypothetical scenarios (65.67%), verbose technical jargon (44.42%)—that 'largely align with those predefined in the system prompt,' creating predictable temporal discourse signatures. Multi-turn jailbreaks succeed through specific persuasion playbooks: rapport THEN reframing THEN escalation, where each step individually appears benign but the sequence constitutes manipulation. Fourth, the Persuasive Fingerprint work (Alizadeh Noughabi et al., CIKM 2025, arXiv 2510.21983) confirmed that Cialdini's persuasion principles computationally characterize jailbreak susceptibility, establishing that persuasion structure is the right analytical lens for understanding jailbreaks. The specific representational gap, now mechanistically grounded by Bullwinkel et al.: DeepContext's generic drift features operate at the same representation level where multi-turn attacks deliberately hide—they see 'the conversation is changing' but cannot distinguish natural evolution (brainstorming, creative collaboration) from adversarial manipulation (rapport→reframing→escalation) because both produce benign-looking per-turn representations. Persuasion-grounded features operate at the discourse level, seeing the specific manipulation playbook being executed even when each turn's semantic representation appears benign. This distinction should enable three concrete improvements: (a) earlier detection—persuasion strategy transitions (e.g., shift from rapport to authority framing) manifest before any explicitly harmful content appears, creating a detection window invisible to drift-only or representation-level systems; (b) lower false-positive rates—benign conversations that naturally drift (high drift magnitude) use qualitatively different persuasion distributions than adversarial conversations (no escalation pattern, no strategic framing shifts); (c) native interpretability—detected threats are explained in terms of named persuasion strategies and their temporal sequence rather than opaque drift magnitudes. GLiGuard's success with single-turn strategy detection validates that compact encoders can learn strategy-level features; DisPeD extends this to the temporal domain. DeepContext's own future-work section identifies 'dual-stream architecture to separate benign functional intent from adversarial steering' as a promising direction—DisPeD directly pursues this line with the critical addition of persuasion grounding.
33	
34	## Landscape (SOTA & Limitations)
35	
36	DeepContext (arXiv 2602.16935, Feb 2026): Primary baseline and most directly comparable system. Fine-tuned BERT encoder with task-attention weighted pooling, 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden), hybrid residual connection concatenating projected hidden state with raw embeddings. Tracks generic intent drift. F1=0.84 on multi-turn benchmarks, sub-20ms on T4 GPU. Training: 437K sequences (~20% malicious). Acknowledged limitations: 'glass cannon' effect (overfits to known patterns), false positives in function-calling contexts, no interpretability (opaque 2048-dim hidden state). No adversarial robustness evaluation. Future work identifies dual-stream architecture as promising direction. We candidly acknowledge that DisPeD's Track B (DeBERTa + GRU over semantic embeddings) is architecturally similar to DeepContext with an upgraded encoder; the contribution lies in Track A's persuasion-strategy sequence modeling, the cross-attention fusion, and the empirical demonstration that persuasion-grounded representations outperform generic drift features. Bullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025): 'A Representation Engineering Perspective on the Effectiveness of Multi-Turn Jailbreaks.' Uses representation reading (MLP probes on intermediate activations, 0.997-0.999 test accuracy) and PCA visualization to show that Crescendo keeps model outputs in a 'benign' region of representation space, with harmful classification dropping sharply from k=1 to k=2 turns. Circuit breakers achieve only 54.2% ASR defense on multi-turn (vs. 3.8% on single-turn), a 50-point generalization gap, because Crescendo representations are 'sufficiently different from single-turn representations.' Tested on Llama-3-8B-Instruct and its circuit-breaker variant. Critically important for DisPeD's theoretical argument: this paper mechanistically explains why per-turn representation-level features are insufficient—attacks are designed to keep them benign—and why discourse-level features (persuasion strategy sequences) are needed. DisPeD's approach directly addresses the gap Bullwinkel et al. identify: rather than trying to detect harmful per-turn representations (which multi-turn attacks suppress), we detect the adversarial persuasion playbook operating above the representation level. GLiGuard (arXiv 2605.07982, May 2026): State-of-the-art single-turn guard. 300M-parameter schema-conditioned bidirectional encoder adapted from GLiNER2, detecting 11 jailbreak strategies and 14 harm categories in a single non-autoregressive forward pass. 16x higher throughput and 17x lower latency than 7B decoder guards; competitive F1 with 7-27B models. Single-input only—no temporal modeling—making it complementary to multi-turn approaches. Included as baseline to characterize the single-turn vs. multi-turn detection gap. BIID (arXiv 2509.22732, Sep 2025): Bidirectional intention inference combining forward request analysis with backward response retrospection. Near-0% ASR on MHJ. Training-free but requires target LLM to self-analyze (2+ full LLM reasoning passes per turn). No published latency analysis; we estimate several seconds per turn based on computational requirements of two full LLM forward passes and plan to independently measure this latency as part of evaluation. Strongest known ASR but no interpretability and untested adversarial robustness. G-Guard (arXiv 2507.07146, Jul 2025): Attention-aware GCN over entity graphs via GraphRAG. Merges turns into global graph via entity deduplication, losing temporal ordering. F1: 1.00/0.87 on HarmBench. Acknowledges 'loss of potentially important contextual information' from aggressive node pruning. XGuard-Train (arXiv 2504.13203, Apr 2025): Training-time defense using collaborative multi-agent framework to generate 30K adversarial conversations. 48.3% vulnerability reduction against X-Teaming attacks, 38.5% against other multi-turn jailbreaks, while mostly preserving general capabilities. Fundamentally complementary to inference-time detection: model-specific, requires fine-tuning access, inapplicable to closed-source models. DisPeD and XGuard-Train can be composed—XGuard-Train hardens the model, DisPeD monitors for attacks that evade the hardening. Concurrent work (acknowledged): Rafiei Asl et al. (ODU/UVA, CCI-funded) are developing a defense against persuasion-based multi-turn jailbreaks using reinforcement learning-driven defensive architectures that dynamically adjust model responses. No preprint published as of May 2026. Their prior work—NEXUS (EMNLP 2025) and HarmNet (arXiv 2510.18728)—focuses on attack frameworks. The RL-based approach is fundamentally different from DisPeD: it is a training-time, model-specific defense that modifies model behavior through RL-based response adjustment, requiring fine-tuning access. DisPeD is an inference-time, model-agnostic detector that monitors conversation patterns externally, applicable to closed-source models via API wrapping. The two approaches are complementary: RL-based response adjustment hardens the model; DisPeD detects attacks that evade the hardening and provides interpretable threat intelligence. 'How Do LLMs Persuade?' (arXiv 2508.05625): Linear probes detect persuasion strategies, success trajectories, and personality-adapted persuasion in multi-turn conversations, faster than prompting-based approaches. Not applied to jailbreak detection. Provides theoretical and empirical grounding for DisPeD's core hypothesis. Hagendorff (Nature Communications 2026): 97% ASR from reasoning-model attackers; identifiable persuasion strategies (flattery 84.75%, educational framing 68.56%, hypothetical scenarios 65.67%, technical jargon 44.42%). Demonstrates persuasion playbooks are the mechanism of sophisticated multi-turn attacks. PAP (Zeng et al., ACL 2024): 40 persuasion techniques; Targeted Summarization defense reduces single-turn ASR to 2% but degrades MT-Bench. Single-turn only. Persuasive Fingerprint (Alizadeh Noughabi et al., CIKM 2025, arXiv 2510.21983): Cialdini's 7 principles characterize jailbreak susceptibility. No defense proposed. SafeDream (arXiv 2604.16824, Apr 2026): Safety world model with CUSUM detection; F1=0.891, FPR=7.9%; requires white-box hidden state access. Nasr, Carlini et al. (arXiv 2510.09023, Oct 2025): Systematically bypassed 12 single-input classifiers with >90% ASR under adaptive attack. All 12 tested defenses were single-turn/single-input classifiers. Establishes necessity of robustness evaluation for any classifier-based defense. Guarding the Guardrails (arXiv 2510.13893): Taxonomy of 7 jailbreak strategy families including persuasion, with 1,364 multi-turn Italian dialogues annotated with taxonomy. LLM-based detection via taxonomy-guided prompting. Peak+Accumulation (arXiv 2602.11247): Zero-GPU proxy scoring; 90.8% recall. Defensive M2S (arXiv 2601.00454): Compresses multi-turn to single-turn; 93.8% recall. Constitutional Classifiers (arXiv 2501.18837, Anthropic): Exchange-level classifier; 4.4% ASR; proprietary. MultiBreak (arXiv 2605.01687): 10,389-sample multi-turn benchmark. SoK: Evaluating Jailbreak Guardrails (arXiv 2506.10597, Jun 2025; IEEE S&P 2026): Comprehensive evaluation framework for jailbreak guardrails; taxonomy across six dimensions. FITD (EMNLP 2025): Foot-in-the-door multi-turn attack; demonstrates persuasion structure drives attacks. No published work combines persuasion-grounded turn representations (not generic drift features) with temporal sequence modeling for multi-turn jailbreak detection. The contribution is representational, not architectural.
37	
38	## Proposed Approach
39	
40	We propose DisPeD (Discourse-level Persuasion Detection), honestly framed as persuasion-grounded DeepContext with dual-track modeling, adversarial robustness evaluation, and interpretability. The primary contribution is demonstrating that persuasion-grounded turn representations are fundamentally better features for multi-turn jailbreak detection than generic intent-drift features, validated through rigorous ablation against a reproduced DeepContext baseline. We frame DisPeD's value as a multi-metric Pareto improvement: rather than claiming dominance on any single axis, we demonstrate that persuasion grounding simultaneously advances detection performance, early detection capability, false-positive discrimination, interpretability, and adversarial robustness—a combination no existing system achieves.
41	
42	**Theoretical Grounding from Representation Engineering:**
43	Bullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956) mechanistically explain why generic semantic features are insufficient: multi-turn attacks deliberately keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region of representation space, causing circuit breakers and other representation-level defenses to fail with a 50-point ASR generalization gap. DisPeD addresses this by operating at the discourse level—detecting the adversarial persuasion playbook that orchestrates the conversation—rather than at the per-turn representation level where attacks deliberately hide. The key insight: even when each turn's semantic representation appears benign (as Bullwinkel et al. show it will), the sequence of persuasion strategies across turns (rapport→reframing→escalation) constitutes a detectable manipulation fingerprint.
44	
45	**Positioning Against DeepContext:**
46	We explicitly acknowledge that DisPeD's temporal architecture (encoder + GRU) shares the same paradigm as DeepContext. The contribution is not a new architecture but a new representation. Concretely: we show that grounding the encoder in persuasion strategy classification (Track A) and fusing discrete strategy signals with continuous semantic signals (cross-attention fusion) yields significant improvement over generic intent-drift detection, while the dual-track design provides adversarial robustness and interpretability that single-track architectures lack. The ablation design is structured to isolate each contribution precisely.
47	
48	**Positioning Against XGuard-Train and Concurrent RL-Based Defenses:**
49	XGuard-Train is a training-time, model-specific defense requiring fine-tuning access. The concurrent ODU/UVA project (Rafiei Asl et al., CCI-funded) pursues RL-based defensive architectures that dynamically adjust model responses—also training-time and model-specific. DisPeD is inference-time, model-agnostic, and deployable on closed-source models via API wrapping. DisPeD provides interpretable post-hoc explanations (named persuasion strategy sequences) that neither training-time alignment nor RL-based response adjustment can provide. These approaches are complementary and composable: training-time defenses harden the model; DisPeD monitors conversations for novel attacks that evade the hardening and provides security teams with actionable threat intelligence.
50	
51	**Phase 1 – Persuasion Strategy Annotation (~40 H100-hours):**
52	Develop a persuasion strategy taxonomy grounded in PAP's social-science categories, Hagendorff's empirical findings, and Cialdini's principles. Categories: (1) rapport/flattery, (2) authority/educational framing, (3) hypothetical scenario construction, (4) reframing/normalization, (5) emotional leverage/urgency, (6) identity manipulation/role assignment, (7) incremental escalation, (8) benign/neutral. Annotate turn-level persuasion strategies on existing multi-turn jailbreak datasets (MHJ, XGuard-Train subset, SafeDialBench, MultiBreak) using GPT-4-class models with taxonomy-guided prompting, validated by expert review on a 500-sample stratified subset (targeting >0.75 Cohen's kappa). Generate additional adversarial conversations (~1,000) using reasoning-model attackers (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-14B, Qwen3-30B-A3B) against 3 targets, extracting persuasion labels from attacker CoT traces. Supplement with benign conversations enriched with natural persuasion: tutoring, customer service, brainstorming, debate, and creative collaboration—specifically selected to exhibit high topic drift with benign persuasion distributions for false-positive calibration. Target: ~30K annotated conversations.
53	
54	**Phase 2 – Persuasion-Grounded Encoder (~25 H100-hours):**
55	Fine-tune DeBERTa-v3-base on a multi-task objective: (a) 8-way persuasion strategy classification, and (b) binary jailbreak-intent classification. The multi-task training produces turn embeddings (penultimate-layer [CLS] representation) that encode both semantic content and the specific persuasion mechanism in play, contrasting with DeepContext's BERT embeddings that encode only generic semantic content. DeBERTa-v3-base is chosen over BERT-base for its disentangled attention mechanism, which improves fine-grained token-level discrimination relevant to distinguishing subtle persuasion strategies.
56	
57	**Phase 3 – Dual-Track Temporal Model (~20 H100-hours):**
58	Track A (Interpretable Persuasion Sequence): GRU over discrete persuasion-strategy probability distributions (8-dim per turn) concatenated with per-turn harmfulness scores from a standalone guard (Qwen3Guard or WildGuard). Detects characteristic attack playbooks: rapport→reframing→escalation. Provides human-readable explanations ('Turn 1-3: rapport-building; Turn 4: authority reframing; Turn 5-6: incremental escalation'). Track B (Robust Semantic Content): GRU over continuous DeBERTa turn embeddings concatenated with harmfulness scores. Architecturally similar to DeepContext with DeBERTa; captures semantic evolution beyond fixed taxonomy categories. Cross-Attention Fusion: At each timestep, bidirectional cross-attention between Track A and Track B hidden states allows the tracks to exchange information. Track A informs Track B about the manipulation context of observed semantic shifts (distinguishing benign drift from adversarial steering); Track B provides Track A with semantic grounding to disambiguate persuasion strategies. Final classification via learned late fusion of track outputs.
59	
60	**Mechanistic Hypothesis for Why Persuasion Labels Beat Generic Drift (now grounded in Bullwinkel et al.):**
61	Bullwinkel et al. show that multi-turn attacks keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region—the representation space is adversarially manipulated. Generic drift detection operates in this same manipulated space: it sees 'the conversation is changing' but the direction of change appears benign. Persuasion-aware detection operates at a different level of abstraction: it classifies the discourse function of each turn (rapport-building, authority framing, reframing) rather than its semantic content. Even when a turn's semantic representation is benign (as Bullwinkel et al. show it will be during Crescendo), its discourse function within the conversation can reveal adversarial intent. This enables: (a) earlier detection—detecting rapport→authority framing transition before any harmful content appears (while generic drift and representation-level defenses see only benign signals); (b) better false-positive discrimination—benign conversations that also 'drift' (brainstorming, creative writing, complex tutoring) exhibit flat or random persuasion distributions rather than the escalatory persuasion sequences characteristic of attacks; (c) interpretable explanations—naming the strategy rather than reporting an opaque drift magnitude.
62	
63	**Phase 4 – Comprehensive Ablation-Driven Evaluation (~45 H100-hours):**
64	
65	*Multi-Metric Pareto Evaluation Framework:*
66	We pre-commit to reporting a multi-dimensional comparison table across six axes: (1) F1 detection accuracy, (2) turns-before-compliance (early detection), (3) FPR on benign high-drift conversations, (4) interpretability score (human evaluation), (5) inference latency, and (6) adaptive attack robustness. DisPeD's claimed value is as a Pareto improvement across these dimensions: BIID achieves the strongest ASR reduction but at ~100x latency with no interpretability; DeepContext matches latency but provides no interpretability, no FPR discrimination, and no adversarial robustness; SafeDream achieves early detection but requires white-box hidden state access. Even modest F1 gains become significant when accompanied by clear wins on interpretability, FPR, and early detection. If F1 gains over DeepContext are ≤2 points, the paper's primary narrative shifts to the multi-metric advantages while honestly reporting the F1 result.
67	
68	*Primary Ablation (DeepContext comparison):*
69	(A0) DeepContext reproduction: BERT-base + task-attention pooling + 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden), trained on generic intent-drift labels, targeting reproduction of reported F1=0.84. (A1) Encoder upgrade: DeBERTa-v3-base replacing BERT-base in the DeepContext architecture (same GRU, same drift task), isolating encoder contribution. (A2) Persuasion grounding: DeBERTa-v3-base with multi-task persuasion training in single-track GRU (Track B only), isolating persuasion-representation contribution. (A3) Full DisPeD: dual-track with cross-attention fusion. This four-condition ablation ensures every claimed contribution is empirically justified relative to the most relevant baseline.
70	
71	*Detection performance:* Precision/recall/F1 on MHJ, MultiBreak, SafeDialBench, XGuard-Train test split, and reasoning-model-attacker test set. Per-condition (A0-A3) to trace contribution sources.
72	
73	*Early detection:* Turns before compliance, per condition. Hypothesis: persuasion-grounded conditions (A2, A3) detect earlier because persuasion patterns manifest before harmful content. We pre-commit to reporting the A1-vs-A2 gap on this metric as a primary result, since this most cleanly isolates the value of persuasion grounding independent of encoder quality.
```

```json
      "content": "# Idea Brief: Beyond Intent Drift: Persuasion-Grounded Turn Representations for Multi-Turn Jailbreak Detection\n\n\n- **Idea ID**: `idea_008`\n- **Created**: 2026-05-17\n- **Approved**: 2026-05-17\n- **Source**: foreign:be-idea-gen-0515-5\n- **Level**: top-tier\n- **Target Venues**: EMNLP 2026 (ARR submission deadline: May 25, 2026; commitment deadline: August 2, 2026; conference: October 24-29, Budapest) — LLM Security is listed as a topic area within the main conference, with additional relevance to Discourse and Pragmatics, NLP Applications, and Ethics/Bias/Fairness topic areas\n\n## Seed Topic\n\nTarget: EMNLP 2026, ARR submission deadline 2026-05-25 (11 days from today 2026-05-14). Track: LLM Security.\n\nDirection: LLM safety robustness — defense methods against jailbreaking, adversarial prompt attacks, and safety alignment degradation. In-scope: robust safety training that resists adaptive attacks, automated red-teaming frameworks for systematic vulnerability discovery, safety-aware decoding or filtering at inference time, detecting and mitigating safety degradation when models are fine-tuned or distilled, multi-turn attack defense, tool-use and agentic safety. NOT limited to text — agentic tool-call safety and multi-modal attack surfaces welcome.\n\nContribution type: METHOD ONLY. Must propose a concrete defense algorithm, detection pipeline, or training recipe — not just a new attack or red-teaming dataset. Attack + defense paired contributions OK if defense is the primary contribution.\n\nHARD CONSTRAINTS:\n1. Compute: ≤200 H100-hours total. Safety research is mostly inference-heavy — budget for evaluation across multiple attack suites, not large-scale pretraining.\n2. Time: ≤11 days. Build on existing open safety benchmarks and models (Llama-3-Guard, ShieldGemma, Qwen3, DeepSeek-R1 distills). Use established attack libraries (HarmBench, JailbreakBench, StrongREJECT) for evaluation.\n3. Eval: Attack Success Rate (ASR) reduction on HarmBench, JailbreakBench, AdvBench; safety score on WildGuard/Aegis; utility preservation on MT-Bench/MMLU (defense must not kill helpfulness).\n\nThis is a NEW dedicated track at EMNLP 2026 — high visibility, growing fast, few established defense methods. 2026 research shows reasoning models can jailbreak other models at 97% success; defense lags far behind attack. Look for: inference-time safety filters that adapt to attack patterns, representation-level safety mechanisms (circuit-based), robust alignment under fine-tuning distribution shift, defense against multi-turn and agentic attacks. Avoid: yet another jailbreak attack without defense contribution, prompt-template-only defenses, safety classifiers without adversarial robustness guarantees.\n\n## Core Problem\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks remain one of the most persistent threats to deployed LLMs, distributing adversarial intent across individually benign-seeming messages that exploit conversational dynamics to gradually steer models past safety boundaries. The state-of-the-art dedicated multi-turn detector, DeepContext (arXiv 2602.16935, Feb 2026), models this threat as generic intent drift: it embeds each turn with fine-tuned BERT, applies task-attention weighted pooling, and feeds the resulting sequence through a 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden units) with hybrid residual connections to detect when the conversation trajectory shifts toward harmful territory. This approach achieves F1=0.84 on standard multi-turn benchmarks with sub-20ms inference on a T4 GPU, establishing a strong baseline. However, DeepContext's reliance on undifferentiated semantic drift creates three fundamental limitations. (1) High false-positive rates on naturally drifting conversations: brainstorming sessions, Socratic dialogues, creative writing, and complex tutoring all exhibit substantial topic drift, triggering drift detectors even though no manipulation is occurring. (2) Zero interpretability: the 2048-dimensional GRU hidden state provides an opaque risk score with no actionable threat intelligence—security teams cannot see which manipulation strategy is being employed or at which turn the attack transitions from rapport-building to exploitation. (3) The acknowledged 'glass cannon' effect—strong performance on known attack templates but fragile generalization to novel strategies—because the model learns surface-level correlates of attacks (lexical drift patterns) rather than the underlying causal mechanism (adversarial persuasion). A recent representation engineering analysis (Bullwinkel et al., arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025) provides a mechanistic explanation for why per-turn representation-level defenses fundamentally struggle with multi-turn attacks: Crescendo keeps model outputs in a 'benign' region of representation space, with harmful classification dropping sharply after just 2 turns. Circuit breakers—trained to reroute harmful single-turn representations—achieve only 54.2% ASR defense (vs. 3.8% on single-turn) because multi-turn attack representations are 'sufficiently different from single-turn representations.' This finding demonstrates that the information needed to detect multi-turn attacks is not reliably present in per-turn representations of semantic content; it resides instead in the discourse-level pattern of how turns relate to each other—specifically, in the adversarial persuasion playbook orchestrating the conversation. Meanwhile, BIID (arXiv 2509.22732) achieves near-0% ASR through bidirectional LLM-based intention inference, but imposes 2+ full LLM reasoning passes per turn with latency estimated at several seconds (no published latency analysis exists; this estimate is based on the computational requirements of two full LLM forward passes per turn and requires independent verification). GLiGuard (arXiv 2605.07982, May 2026) demonstrates that compact 300M-parameter encoders can detect 11 jailbreak strategies and 14 harm categories in a single pass with 16x speedup over 7B decoder guards, but operates only on single inputs without temporal modeling. XGuard-Train (arXiv 2504.13203) achieves 48.3% vulnerability reduction through safety training data (30K multi-turn conversations), but as a training-time defense, it is model-specific and inapplicable to closed-source or already-deployed models. The core problem is therefore not architectural—encoder-plus-recurrent designs are well-established for this task—but representational: the features extracted from each turn do not encode the information most relevant to distinguishing adversarial manipulation from benign conversational evolution, and as Bullwinkel et al. show, per-turn semantic representations are actively misleading because multi-turn attacks are designed to keep them benign. What turn-level representation would capture the causal mechanism of multi-turn attacks at the discourse level, above the representation space where attacks deliberately hide?\n\n## Opportunity / Why Now\n\nFour converging findings define a precise representational opportunity. First, Bullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025) established that multi-turn jailbreaks succeed specifically because they keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region of representation space, explaining why representation-level defenses (circuit breakers) fail with a 50-percentage-point generalization gap. This finding has a critical implication: effective multi-turn detection requires features that operate above the per-turn representation level—at the discourse level where the manipulation playbook is visible even when individual turns appear benign. Second, 'How Do LLMs Persuade?' (arXiv 2508.05625) demonstrated that linear probes applied to intermediate transformer representations can reliably detect persuasion strategies, track persuasion success trajectories, and identify personality-adapted persuasion across multi-turn conversations—faster than prompting-based approaches and with competitive accuracy. Although this work was not applied to jailbreak detection, it empirically validates a crucial premise: the specific persuasion mechanisms operating in a conversation are extractable from turn-level representations and computationally tractable. Third, Hagendorff (Nature Communications 2026) showed that reasoning-model attackers achieving 97% ASR employ identifiable persuasion strategies—flattery/rapport-building (84.75%), educational/research framing (68.56%), hypothetical scenarios (65.67%), verbose technical jargon (44.42%)—that 'largely align with those predefined in the system prompt,' creating predictable temporal discourse signatures. Multi-turn jailbreaks succeed through specific persuasion playbooks: rapport THEN reframing THEN escalation, where each step individually appears benign but the sequence constitutes manipulation. Fourth, the Persuasive Fingerprint work (Alizadeh Noughabi et al., CIKM 2025, arXiv 2510.21983) confirmed that Cialdini's persuasion principles computationally characterize jailbreak susceptibility, establishing that persuasion structure is the right analytical lens for understanding jailbreaks. The specific representational gap, now mechanistically grounded by Bullwinkel et al.: DeepContext's generic drift features operate at the same representation level where multi-turn attacks deliberately hide—they see 'the conversation is changing' but cannot distinguish natural evolution (brainstorming, creative collaboration) from adversarial manipulation (rapport→reframing→escalation) because both produce benign-looking per-turn representations. Persuasion-grounded features operate at the discourse level, seeing the specific manipulation playbook being executed even when each turn's semantic representation appears benign. This distinction should enable three concrete improvements: (a) earlier detection—persuasion strategy transitions (e.g., shift from rapport to authority framing) manifest before any explicitly harmful content appears, creating a detection window invisible to drift-only or representation-level systems; (b) lower false-positive rates—benign conversations that naturally drift (high drift magnitude) use qualitatively different persuasion distributions than adversarial conversations (no escalation pattern, no strategic framing shifts); (c) native interpretability—detected threats are explained in terms of named persuasion strategies and their temporal sequence rather than opaque drift magnitudes. GLiGuard's success with single-turn strategy detection validates that compact encoders can learn strategy-level features; DisPeD extends this to the temporal domain. DeepContext's own future-work section identifies 'dual-stream architecture to separate benign functional intent from adversarial steering' as a promising direction—DisPeD directly pursues this line with the critical addition of persuasion grounding.\n\n## Landscape (SOTA & Limitations)\n\nDeepContext (arXiv 2602.16935, Feb 2026): Primary baseline and most directly comparable system. Fine-tuned BERT encoder with task-attention weighted pooling, 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden), hybrid residual connection concatenating projected hidden state with raw embeddings. Tracks generic intent drift. F1=0.84 on multi-turn benchmarks, sub-20ms on T4 GPU. Training: 437K sequences (~20% malicious). Acknowledged limitations: 'glass cannon' effect (overfits to known patterns), false positives in function-calling contexts, no interpretability (opaque 2048-dim hidden state). No adversarial robustness evaluation. Future work identifies dual-stream architecture as promising direction. We candidly acknowledge that DisPeD's Track B (DeBERTa + GRU over semantic embeddings) is architecturally similar to DeepContext with an upgraded encoder; the contribution lies in Track A's persuasion-strategy sequence modeling, the cross-attention fusion, and the empirical demonstration that persuasion-grounded representations outperform generic drift features. Bullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025): 'A Representation Engineering Perspective on the Effectiveness of Multi-Turn Jailbreaks.' Uses representation reading (MLP probes on intermediate activations, 0.997-0.999 test accuracy) and PCA visualization to show that Crescendo keeps model outputs in a 'benign' region of representation space, with harmful classification dropping sharply from k=1 to k=2 turns. Circuit breakers achieve only 54.2% ASR defense on multi-turn (vs. 3.8% on single-turn), a 50-point generalization gap, because Crescendo representations are 'sufficiently different from single-turn representations.' Tested on Llama-3-8B-Instruct and its circuit-breaker variant. Critically important for DisPeD's theoretical argument: this paper mechanistically explains why per-turn representation-level features are insufficient—attacks are designed to keep them benign—and why discourse-level features (persuasion strategy sequences) are needed. DisPeD's approach directly addresses the gap Bullwinkel et al. identify: rather than trying to detect harmful per-turn representations (which multi-turn attacks suppress), we detect the adversarial persuasion playbook operating above the representation level. GLiGuard (arXiv 2605.07982, May 2026): State-of-the-art single-turn guard. 300M-parameter schema-conditioned bidirectional encoder adapted from GLiNER2, detecting 11 jailbreak strategies and 14 harm categories in a single non-autoregressive forward pass. 16x higher throughput and 17x lower latency than 7B decoder guards; competitive F1 with 7-27B models. Single-input only—no temporal modeling—making it complementary to multi-turn approaches. Included as baseline to characterize the single-turn vs. multi-turn detection gap. BIID (arXiv 2509.22732, Sep 2025): Bidirectional intention inference combining forward request analysis with backward response retrospection. Near-0% ASR on MHJ. Training-free but requires target LLM to self-analyze (2+ full LLM reasoning passes per turn). No published latency analysis; we estimate several seconds per turn based on computational requirements of two full LLM forward passes and plan to independently measure this latency as part of evaluation. Strongest known ASR but no interpretability and untested adversarial robustness. G-Guard (arXiv 2507.07146, Jul 2025): Attention-aware GCN over entity graphs via GraphRAG. Merges turns into global graph via entity deduplication, losing temporal ordering. F1: 1.00/0.87 on HarmBench. Acknowledges 'loss of potentially important contextual information' from aggressive node pruning. XGuard-Train (arXiv 2504.13203, Apr 2025): Training-time defense using collaborative multi-agent framework to generate 30K adversarial conversations. 48.3% vulnerability reduction against X-Teaming attacks, 38.5% against other multi-turn jailbreaks, while mostly preserving general capabilities. Fundamentally complementary to inference-time detection: model-specific, requires fine-tuning access, inapplicable to closed-source models. DisPeD and XGuard-Train can be composed—XGuard-Train hardens the model, DisPeD monitors for attacks that evade the hardening. Concurrent work (acknowledged): Rafiei Asl et al. (ODU/UVA, CCI-funded) are developing a defense against persuasion-based multi-turn jailbreaks using reinforcement learning-driven defensive architectures that dynamically adjust model responses. No preprint published as of May 2026. Their prior work—NEXUS (EMNLP 2025) and HarmNet (arXiv 2510.18728)—focuses on attack frameworks. The RL-based approach is fundamentally different from DisPeD: it is a training-time, model-specific defense that modifies model behavior through RL-based response adjustment, requiring fine-tuning access. DisPeD is an inference-time, model-agnostic detector that monitors conversation patterns externally, applicable to closed-source models via API wrapping. The two approaches are complementary: RL-based response adjustment hardens the model; DisPeD detects attacks that evade the hardening and provides interpretable threat intelligence. 'How Do LLMs Persuade?' (arXiv 2508.05625): Linear probes detect persuasion strategies, success trajectories, and personality-adapted persuasion in multi-turn conversations, faster than prompting-based approaches. Not applied to jailbreak detection. Provides theoretical and empirical grounding for DisPeD's core hypothesis. Hagendorff (Nature Communications 2026): 97% ASR from reasoning-model attackers; identifiable persuasion strategies (flattery 84.75%, educational framing 68.56%, hypothetical scenarios 65.67%, technical jargon 44.42%). Demonstrates persuasion playbooks are the mechanism of sophisticated multi-turn attacks. PAP (Zeng et al., ACL 2024): 40 persuasion techniques; Targeted Summarization defense reduces single-turn ASR to 2% but degrades MT-Bench. Single-turn only. Persuasive Fingerprint (Alizadeh Noughabi et al., CIKM 2025, arXiv 2510.21983): Cialdini's 7 principles characterize jailbreak susceptibility. No defense proposed. SafeDream (arXiv 2604.16824, Apr 2026): Safety world model with CUSUM detection; F1=0.891, FPR=7.9%; requires white-box hidden state access. Nasr, Carlini et al. (arXiv 2510.09023, Oct 2025): Systematically bypassed 12 single-input classifiers with >90% ASR under adaptive attack. All 12 tested defenses were single-turn/single-input classifiers. Establishes necessity of robustness evaluation for any classifier-based defense. Guarding the Guardrails (arXiv 2510.13893): Taxonomy of 7 jailbreak strategy families including persuasion, with 1,364 multi-turn Italian dialogues annotated with taxonomy. LLM-based detection via taxonomy-guided prompting. Peak+Accumulation (arXiv 2602.11247): Zero-GPU proxy scoring; 90.8% recall. Defensive M2S (arXiv 2601.00454): Compresses multi-turn to single-turn; 93.8% recall. Constitutional Classifiers (arXiv 2501.18837, Anthropic): Exchange-level classifier; 4.4% ASR; proprietary. MultiBreak (arXiv 2605.01687): 10,389-sample multi-turn benchmark. SoK: Evaluating Jailbreak Guardrails (arXiv 2506.10597, Jun 2025; IEEE S&P 2026): Comprehensive evaluation framework for jailbreak guardrails; taxonomy across six dimensions. FITD (EMNLP 2025): Foot-in-the-door multi-turn attack; demonstrates persuasion structure drives attacks. No published work combines persuasion-grounded turn representations (not generic drift features) with temporal sequence modeling for multi-turn jailbreak detection. The contribution is representational, not architectural.\n\n## Proposed Approach\n\nWe propose DisPeD (Discourse-level Persuasion Detection), honestly framed as persuasion-grounded DeepContext with dual-track modeling, adversarial robustness evaluation, and interpretability. The primary contribution is demonstrating that persuasion-grounded turn representations are fundamentally better features for multi-turn jailbreak detection than generic intent-drift features, validated through rigorous ablation against a reproduced DeepContext baseline. We frame DisPeD's value as a multi-metric Pareto improvement: rather than claiming dominance on any single axis, we demonstrate that persuasion grounding simultaneously advances detection performance, early detection capability, false-positive discrimination, interpretability, and adversarial robustness—a combination no existing system achieves.\n\n**Theoretical Grounding from Representation Engineering:**\nBullwinkel et al. (arXiv 2507.02956) mechanistically explain why generic semantic features are insufficient: multi-turn attacks deliberately keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region of representation space, causing circuit breakers and other representation-level defenses to fail with a 50-point ASR generalization gap. DisPeD addresses this by operating at the discourse level—detecting the adversarial persuasion playbook that orchestrates the conversation—rather than at the per-turn representation level where attacks deliberately hide. The key insight: even when each turn's semantic representation appears benign (as Bullwinkel et al. show it will), the sequence of persuasion strategies across turns (rapport→reframing→escalation) constitutes a detectable manipulation fingerprint.\n\n**Positioning Against DeepContext:**\nWe explicitly acknowledge that DisPeD's temporal architecture (encoder + GRU) shares the same paradigm as DeepContext. The contribution is not a new architecture but a new representation. Concretely: we show that grounding the encoder in persuasion strategy classification (Track A) and fusing discrete strategy signals with continuous semantic signals (cross-attention fusion) yields significant improvement over generic intent-drift detection, while the dual-track design provides adversarial robustness and interpretability that single-track architectures lack. The ablation design is structured to isolate each contribution precisely.\n\n**Positioning Against XGuard-Train and Concurrent RL-Based Defenses:**\nXGuard-Train is a training-time, model-specific defense requiring fine-tuning access. The concurrent ODU/UVA project (Rafiei Asl et al., CCI-funded) pursues RL-based defensive architectures that dynamically adjust model responses—also training-time and model-specific. DisPeD is inference-time, model-agnostic, and deployable on closed-source models via API wrapping. DisPeD provides interpretable post-hoc explanations (named persuasion strategy sequences) that neither training-time alignment nor RL-based response adjustment can provide. These approaches are complementary and composable: training-time defenses harden the model; DisPeD monitors conversations for novel attacks that evade the hardening and provides security teams with actionable threat intelligence.\n\n**Phase 1 – Persuasion Strategy Annotation (~40 H100-hours):**\nDevelop a persuasion strategy taxonomy grounded in PAP's social-science categories, Hagendorff's empirical findings, and Cialdini's principles. Categories: (1) rapport/flattery, (2) authority/educational framing, (3) hypothetical scenario construction, (4) reframing/normalization, (5) emotional leverage/urgency, (6) identity manipulation/role assignment, (7) incremental escalation, (8) benign/neutral. Annotate turn-level persuasion strategies on existing multi-turn jailbreak datasets (MHJ, XGuard-Train subset, SafeDialBench, MultiBreak) using GPT-4-class models with taxonomy-guided prompting, validated by expert review on a 500-sample stratified subset (targeting >0.75 Cohen's kappa). Generate additional adversarial conversations (~1,000) using reasoning-model attackers (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-14B, Qwen3-30B-A3B) against 3 targets, extracting persuasion labels from attacker CoT traces. Supplement with benign conversations enriched with natural persuasion: tutoring, customer service, brainstorming, debate, and creative collaboration—specifically selected to exhibit high topic drift with benign persuasion distributions for false-positive calibration. Target: ~30K annotated conversations.\n\n**Phase 2 – Persuasion-Grounded Encoder (~25 H100-hours):**\nFine-tune DeBERTa-v3-base on a multi-task objective: (a) 8-way persuasion strategy classification, and (b) binary jailbreak-intent classification. The multi-task training produces turn embeddings (penultimate-layer [CLS] representation) that encode both semantic content and the specific persuasion mechanism in play, contrasting with DeepContext's BERT embeddings that encode only generic semantic content. DeBERTa-v3-base is chosen over BERT-base for its disentangled attention mechanism, which improves fine-grained token-level discrimination relevant to distinguishing subtle persuasion strategies.\n\n**Phase 3 – Dual-Track Temporal Model (~20 H100-hours):**\nTrack A (Interpretable Persuasion Sequence): GRU over discrete persuasion-strategy probability distributions (8-dim per turn) concatenated with per-turn harmfulness scores from a standalone guard (Qwen3Guard or WildGuard). Detects characteristic attack playbooks: rapport→reframing→escalation. Provides human-readable explanations ('Turn 1-3: rapport-building; Turn 4: authority reframing; Turn 5-6: incremental escalation'). Track B (Robust Semantic Content): GRU over continuous DeBERTa turn embeddings concatenated with harmfulness scores. Architecturally similar to DeepContext with DeBERTa; captures semantic evolution beyond fixed taxonomy categories. Cross-Attention Fusion: At each timestep, bidirectional cross-attention between Track A and Track B hidden states allows the tracks to exchange information. Track A informs Track B about the manipulation context of observed semantic shifts (distinguishing benign drift from adversarial steering); Track B provides Track A with semantic grounding to disambiguate persuasion strategies. Final classification via learned late fusion of track outputs.\n\n**Mechanistic Hypothesis for Why Persuasion Labels Beat Generic Drift (now grounded in Bullwinkel et al.):**\nBullwinkel et al. show that multi-turn attacks keep per-turn representations in a 'benign' region—the representation space is adversarially manipulated. Generic drift detection operates in this same manipulated space: it sees 'the conversation is changing' but the direction of change appears benign. Persuasion-aware detection operates at a different level of abstraction: it classifies the discourse function of each turn (rapport-building, authority framing, reframing) rather than its semantic content. Even when a turn's semantic representation is benign (as Bullwinkel et al. show it will be during Crescendo), its discourse function within the conversation can reveal adversarial intent. This enables: (a) earlier detection—detecting rapport→authority framing transition before any harmful content appears (while generic drift and representation-level defenses see only benign signals); (b) better false-positive discrimination—benign conversations that also 'drift' (brainstorming, creative writing, complex tutoring) exhibit flat or random persuasion distributions rather than the escalatory persuasion sequences characteristic of attacks; (c) interpretable explanations—naming the strategy rather than reporting an opaque drift magnitude.\n\n**Phase 4 – Comprehensive Ablation-Driven Evaluation (~45 H100-hours):**\n\n*Multi-Metric Pareto Evaluation Framework:*\nWe pre-commit to reporting a multi-dimensional comparison table across six axes: (1) F1 detection accuracy, (2) turns-before-compliance (early detection), (3) FPR on benign high-drift conversations, (4) interpretability score (human evaluation), (5) inference latency, and (6) adaptive attack robustness. DisPeD's claimed value is as a Pareto improvement across these dimensions: BIID achieves the strongest ASR reduction but at ~100x latency with no interpretability; DeepContext matches latency but provides no interpretability, no FPR discrimination, and no adversarial robustness; SafeDream achieves early detection but requires white-box hidden state access. Even modest F1 gains become significant when accompanied by clear wins on interpretability, FPR, and early detection. If F1 gains over DeepContext are ≤2 points, the paper's primary narrative shifts to the multi-metric advantages while honestly reporting the F1 result.\n\n*Primary Ablation (DeepContext comparison):*\n(A0) DeepContext reproduction: BERT-base + task-attention pooling + 3-layer GRU (2048 hidden), trained on generic intent-drift labels, targeting reproduction of reported F1=0.84. (A1) Encoder upgrade: DeBERTa-v3-base replacing BERT-base in the DeepContext architecture (same GRU, same drift task), isolating encoder contribution. (A2) Persuasion grounding: DeBERTa-v3-base with multi-task persuasion training in single-track GRU (Track B only), isolating persuasion-representation contribution. (A3) Full DisPeD: dual-track with cross-attention fusion. This four-condition ablation ensures every claimed contribution is empirically justified relative to the most relevant baseline.\n\n*Detection performance:* Precision/recall/F1 on MHJ, MultiBreak, SafeDialBench, XGuard-Train test split, and reasoning-model-attacker test set. Per-condition (A0-A3) to trace contribution sources.\n\n*Early detection:* Turns before compliance, per condition. Hypothesis: persuasion-grounded conditions (A2, A3) detect earlier because persuasion patterns manifest before harmful content. We pre-commit to reporting the A1-vs-A2 gap on this metric as a primary result, since this most cleanly isolates the value of persuasion grounding independent of encoder quality.\n\n*False-p\n\n## Resource Estimate\n\n- **auto_research**: {'claude_api_cost': '$250-400 (heavy orchestration: annotation pipeline, 4-condition ablation management, multi-benchmark evaluation, adaptive attack generation across ~4000-6000 turns)', 'estimated_gpu_cost': '$400-500', 'gpu_hours': '~160h H100 (broader annotation coverage, full 4-condition ablation with multiple seeds, comprehensive adaptive attack evaluation, all benchmark evaluations, latency benchmarking, GLiGuard baseline)', 'gpu_utilization': 'High (~75-85%): continuous experiment scheduling across annotation, training, and evaluation pipelines', 'risk_note': \"Primary risk: if DeepContext reproduction fails or if persuasion-grounded representations show only marginal improvement (<=2 F1 points) over encoder-upgraded DeepContext (A1 vs A2), the paper reframes around multi-metric Pareto improvement. Pre-committed secondary metrics (turns-before-compliance, FPR reduction, interpretability) are expected to show clearer advantages even if F1 gains are modest, since persuasion patterns manifest before harmful content (early detection) and benign conversations lack escalatory persuasion sequences (FPR). Contingency: if MVE (8 days) shows persuasion grounding has no advantage on ANY metric, this constitutes a well-documented negative result with theoretical analysis of why (contributing to the field's understanding of multi-turn detection representations).\", 'speed_bottleneck': 'DeepContext reproduction without code release; sequential multi-turn conversation generation (~50-100s/conversation); API rate limits for closed-source target evaluation', 'team': '0 (initial setup: persuasion taxonomy, DeepContext reproduction script, benchmark selection, adaptive attack specifications)', 'timeline_tiered': {'full_experiment': '11 days with 8x H100 cluster — A0-A3 on 5+ benchmarks, 3 seeds, ~30K annotations, adaptive attacks, all baselines, interpretability evaluation', 'minimum_viable_experiment': '8 days with 8x H100 cluster — A0+A3 on MHJ+MultiBreak, single seed, ~15K annotations, core metrics (F1, turns-before-compliance, FPR, latency)', 'phase_overlap': 'Annotation (days 1-4), encoder training (days 3-6, using early batches), DeepContext reproduction (days 1-5, parallel), dual-track training (days 5-8), evaluation (days 7-11 with MVE conditions first)'}}\n- **human_in_loop**: {'claude_api_cost': '$150-250 (experiment orchestration, annotation assistance, ablation analysis across ~1500-2500 turns)', 'estimated_gpu_cost': '$350-425', 'gpu_hours': '~140h H100 (agent optimizes annotation quality, encoder hyperparameters, cross-attention design, DeepContext reproduction fidelity, adaptive attack design)', 'gpu_utilization': 'Moderate-High (~60-70%): agent queues experiments; human reviews persuasion taxonomy, annotation agreement metrics, and ablation results ~1x/day', 'risk_note': 'DeepContext reproduction is critical path: if we cannot reproduce F1=0.84 (paper provides no code release), the ablation story weakens. Mitigation: contact authors for implementation details; if reproduction gap >3 F1 points, report both reproduced and original numbers with discussion of discrepancy, and pivot to A2-vs-A3 comparison. Persuasion annotation ambiguity estimated at ~10-15% at strategy boundaries; mitigated by multi-task training that uses both discrete labels and continuous embeddings.', 'team': '1 researcher, ~30min/day reviewing persuasion annotations, DeepContext reproduction fidelity, ablation deltas, and interpretability outputs', 'timeline': '4-5 weeks'}\n- **manual**: {'estimated_gpu_cost': '$325-400 at ~$2.50/hr H100', 'gpu_hours': '~130h H100 (persuasion annotation with GPT-4-class models: ~40h, DeBERTa multi-task training: ~25h, dual-track GRU training: ~20h, comprehensive ablation evaluation with DeepContext reproduction + baselines: ~45h)', 'gpu_utilization': 'Moderate (~50-60%): annotation is sequential per conversation; training is standard fine-tuning; evaluation is parallelizable across conditions', 'team': '2 researchers (1 NLP/discourse analysis lead, 1 safety/ML engineer) + 3 security practitioners for interpretability evaluation (4-6 hours each)', 'timeline': '2-3 months'}\n\n## Key References\n\n- DeepContext: Stateful Real-Time Detection of Multi-Turn Adversarial Intent Drift in LLMs (arXiv 2602.16935, Feb 2026) — PRIMARY BASELINE: BERT+GRU intent-drift detector, F1=0.84, sub-20ms T4\n- A Representation Engineering Perspective on the Effectiveness of Multi-Turn Jailbreaks (Bullwinkel et al., arXiv 2507.02956, Microsoft Research, Jun 2025) — shows multi-turn attacks keep representations 'benign', circuit breakers fail with 50-point gap; THEORETICAL FOUNDATION for discourse-level features\n- GLiGuard: Schema-Conditioned Classification for LLM Safeguard (arXiv 2605.07982, May 2026) — 300M encoder, 11 jailbreak strategies + 14 harm categories, single-turn only, 16x faster than 7B guards\n- How Do LLMs Persuade? Linear Probes Can Uncover Persuasion Dynamics in Multi-Turn Conversations (arXiv 2508.05625) — validates persuasion dynamics are extractable from turn-level representations\n- Large reasoning models are autonomous jailbreak agents (Hagendorff et al., Nature Communications 2026) — 97% ASR via identifiable persuasion strategies\n- Bidirectional Intention Inference Enhances LLMs' Defense Against Multi-Turn Jailbreak Attacks (BIID, arXiv 2509.22732, Sep 2025) — near-0% ASR, no published latency analysis\n- X-Teaming: Multi-Turn Jailbreaks and Defenses with Adaptive Multi-Agents / XGuard-Train (arXiv 2504.13203, Apr 2025) — training-time defense, 30K conversations, 48.3% vulnerability reduction\n- Attention-Aware GNN-based Input Defense against Multi-Turn LLM Jailbreak (G-Guard, arXiv 2507.07146, Jul 2025)\n- How Johnny Can Persuade LLMs to Jailbreak Them (PAP, Zeng et al., ACL 2024) — 40 persuasion techniques, single-turn\n- Uncovering the Persuasive Fingerprint of LLMs in Jailbreaking Attacks (Alizadeh Noughabi et al., CIKM 2025, arXiv 2510.21983) — Cialdini's principles for jailbreak analysis\n- The Attacker Moves Second (Nasr, Carlini et al., arXiv 2510.09023, Oct 2025) — adaptive attacks bypass 12 single-input classifiers with >90% ASR\n- Intention Analysis Makes LLMs A Good Jailbreak Defender (Zhang et al., COLING 2025, arXiv 2401.06561)\n- SafeDream: Safety World Model for Proactive Early Jailbreak Detection (arXiv 2604.16824, Apr 2026)\n- Guarding the Guardrails: A Taxonomy-Driven Approach to Jailbreak Detection (arXiv 2510.13893) — 7 jailbreak strategy families, multi-turn Italian dataset\n- Peak + Accumulation: Proxy-Level Scoring for Multi-Turn LLM Attack Detection (arXiv 2602.11247, Feb 2026)\n- Defensive M2S: Training Guardrail Models on Compressed Multi-turn Conversations (arXiv 2601.00454, Jan 2026)",
```

````text
## Worker 操作规范

### ssh-env-setup

# 远程服务器环境搭建规范

## 核心原则

**先探测，再安装。** 不要假设服务器上什么都没有——很多包已经装好了。安装前必须走 discovery 流程。

**镜像快速路径**：连接服务器后，先用 `detect_image` 检测是否为预置镜像。如果是已知镜像且版本匹配，跳过基础环境搭建（Phase 1-4），直接安装项目特有依赖。未检测到镜像时走传统流程。

**aml-train 镜像使用 conda 环境**（不是 venv）。环境激活：`source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base`。pip/python 在 `/root/miniconda3/bin/` 下。**不要假设 .venv 存在，不要创建 venv。**

## Phase 1: 环境探测（用 ssh_read_only）

连接到服务器后，第一步永远是收集信息，不要直接装东西：

```bash
# 1. Python 环境
which python3 && python3 --version
pip3 --version 2>/dev/null || echo "NO_PIP"

# 2. CUDA 环境
nvidia-smi --query-gpu=name,memory.total,driver_version --format=csv,noheader 2>/dev/null || echo "NO_GPU"
nvcc --version 2>/dev/null | grep "release" || echo "NO_NVCC"

# 3. 包管理器
ls -d /opt/miniconda3 ~/miniconda3 ~/.conda 2>/dev/null || echo "NO_CONDA"
ls -d .venv venv env 2>/dev/null || echo "NO_VENV"
pip3 list --format=freeze 2>/dev/null | grep -iE "torch|transformers|flash" || echo "NO_ML_PKGS"

# 4. PEP 668 检查
python3 -c "import sysconfig; print(sysconfig.get_path('stdlib'))" 2>/dev/null
ls /usr/lib/python*/EXTERNALLY-MANAGED 2>/dev/null && echo "PEP668_MANAGED" || echo "PEP668_OK"
```

根据探测结果决定安装策略，不要猜。

## Phase 2: 环境策略选择

根据 Phase 1 结果选择**一个**策略，不要混用：

| 探测结果 | 策略 | 说明 |
|----------|------|------|
| 项目目录有 `.venv/` | 用它：`source .venv/bin/activate` | 最优先 |
| 有 conda 且有写权限 | `conda activate base` 或创建专属 env | 次优先 |
| PEP668_MANAGED + 无 venv | 创建 venv：`python3 -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate` | 不要用 --break-system-packages |
| PEP668_OK（无保护） | 直接 `pip install`（旧系统） | 仍建议 venv |

**决不混用 conda install + pip install 到同一环境。** 选了 pip 就全用 pip。

## Phase 3: PyTorch 安装

### CUDA 版本映射（2025-2026 常用）

先从 `nvcc --version` 或 `nvidia-smi` 获取 CUDA 版本，然后匹配：

| CUDA 版本 | PyTorch pip index URL | 推荐 torch 版本 |
|-----------|----------------------|-----------------|
| 11.8 | `https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu118` | ≥ 2.0 |
| 12.1 | `https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu121` | ≥ 2.1 |
| 12.4 | `https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu124` | ≥ 2.4 |
| 12.6 | `https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu126` | ≥ 2.6 |

**不要猜版本号。** 如果不确定可用版本：
```bash
pip index versions torch --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu121 2>/dev/null | head -3
```

安装命令：
```bash
pip install torch torchvision torchaudio --index-url https://download.pytorch.org/whl/cu{CUDA_SHORT}
```
````

````text
# exp17_dataset_scale 诊断报告

**诊断时间**: 2026-05-19 ~07:50 CST
**服务器**: westd-33183

## 根本原因

**Checkpoint 格式不匹配**：`train_deberta_mlm.py` 保存为 HuggingFace safetensors 格式 (`model.safetensors`)，但 `train_classifier.py --mode treatment` 的 `load_encoder()` 调用 `torch.load()` 期望 `model.pt`。导致所有 MLM Stage2 均 crash：

```
FileNotFoundError: [Errno 2] No such file or directory: 'checkpoints/exp17/mlm_seed42/deberta_mlm/best/model.pt'
```

实际文件为 `model.safetensors`（735MB），路径正确但格式不对。

## 各任务状态

### Vanilla (GPU2) — ✅ 全部完成
| Seed | Val Loss | Val Acc | 状态 |
|------|----------|---------|------|
| 42   | 0.0679   | 0.9733  | ✅ |
| 123  | 0.0594   | 0.9933  | ✅ |
| 456  | (见 results/) | ✅ | ✅ |

Checkpoints: `checkpoints/exp17/vanilla_seed{42,123,456}/baseline/best.pt`

### 9-class (GPU3) — ✅ 完成但有异常

原始脚本在 Stage1 seed=42 时进程中断（仅记录到 model loading）。Resume 脚本补完了全部 3 seeds。

| Seed | Stage1 | Stage2 Val Acc | 备注 |
|------|--------|----------------|------|
| 42   | ✅ | 1.0000（degenerate） | ⚠️ Val Loss=0.0000，所有 epoch 均为 0.0/1.0 |
| 123  | ✅ (Persuasion Acc=0.82, Intent Acc=0.99) | 需确认 | 正常 |
| 456  | ✅ | 0.9933 | Val Loss 0.0004→0.0076 稳定 |

⚠️ **seed=42 Stage2 结果退化**：train_loss 从 0.0453 骤降到 0.0000，val_loss 始终 0.0000。可能原因：
1. 原始 Stage1 进程中断，checkpoint 可能不完整（但文件存在）
2. 9-class multitask embeddings 对 binary 任务过于 informative，GRU 直接 memorize

Checkpoints: `checkpoints/exp17/9class_seed{42,123,456}/` (multitask + treatment)

### MLM (GPU0/1) — 🔄 修复中

原始 MLM Stage2 全部因 model.pt/safetensors 不匹配而 crash。修复脚本已启动在 GPU 0/1：

| Seed | Stage1 | Stage2 | 当前状态 |
|------|--------|--------|----------|
| 42   | ✅ (val_loss=4.0762) | 🔄 running on GPU0 | fix 脚本 Stage2 val_acc=0.9800 (epoch 19) |
| 123  | 🔄 running on GPU0 | pending | fix 脚本 Stage1 epoch 4/4 进行中 |
--- scrambled ---
# Scrambled 模型训练数据差异调查报告

## 三次实验时间线

| 实验 | 数据源 | Benign 来源 | DD OOD F1 | Perturbation F1 |
|------|--------|-------------|-----------|-----------------|
| plan_003_v2 (原始) | plan_002_splits 等价数据 | topic-anchored-v2 | 0.981 (plan_006 eval) | N/A |
| plan_003_scrambled_retrain | **final_v2** | **v4c-casual** | N/A (未跑DD OOD) | **0.333** (全预测benign) |
| plan_003_scrambled_fix | plan_002_splits | topic-anchored-v2 | **0.990** | 1.000 |

注：plan_003_scrambled_retrain 的结果文件 (`plan_003_scrambled_perturbation.json`) 中的 `clean_results` 和 `perturbed_results` 是在 `plan_004_perturbed_test.jsonl`（benign=topic-anchored-v2）上的评估，不是 DD OOD。但效果等价——模型在训练分布外的 benign 数据上完全崩溃。

## 数据集对比

### 样本数

| 数据集 | train | val | test | train_scrambled |
|--------|-------|-----|------|-----------------|
| final_v2 | 336 (175 jb + 161 bn) | 71 | 73 | 336 |
| plan_002_splits | 350 (175 jb + 175 bn) | 74 | 76 | 350 |

### Benign 数据来源

| 属性 | v4c-casual (final_v2) | topic-anchored-v2 (plan_002) | jailbreak (crescendo) |
|------|----------------------|------------------------------|----------------------|
| source | qwen3-8b-v4c-casual | qwen3-8b-topic-anchored-v2 | crescendo |
| 对话结构 | **user-only** | user+assistant | user+assistant |
| 有 assistant 轮次 | **0/161** | **175/175** | 175/175 |
| 平均 user 轮数 | **6.5** | 8.1 | 8.3 |
````

```text
meta:
  venue: EMNLP 2026
  track: LLM Security / Discourse and Pragmatics
  submission_system: ARR May 2026 cycle
  template: acl2023
  template_note: "EMNLP 2026 uses ARR; ACL 2023+ style files apply."
  page_limit: 8
  deadline: "2026-05-25"
  core_claim: >
    Systematic ablation reveals that domain-adaptive continued pretraining on
    jailbreak conversation text (even MLM without classification labels) bridges
    the entire +72pp OOD gap (vanilla F1=0.28 → MLM F1=1.0). Classification
    supervision, label quality, label granularity, temporal modeling, and loss
    weighting are all non-factors. The persuasion taxonomy's sole surviving
    value is interpretability via strategy trajectory analysis.
  created_at: "2026-05-18T00:00:00Z"
  updated_at: "2026-05-18T00:00:00Z"
  reframe: "D019 — MLM control falsified persuasion-aware claim"
  title: "What Makes Multi-Turn Jailbreaks Detectable? Domain Exposure Bridges the Cross-Attack Gap"

paper_outline:
  - section: abstract
    word_budget: 220
    core_bullets:
      - "Problem: Multi-turn jailbreaks distribute adversarial intent across benign turns; vanilla semantic temporal modeling collapses on unseen attack types (F1=0.28 on DD)"
      - "Approach: Systematic ablation decomposing what enables cross-attack generalization — we expected persuasion-specific classification to be key"
      - "Surprise finding: MLM continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text (no classification labels) bridges the entire +72pp OOD gap (F1=1.0)"
      - "Evidence hierarchy: vanilla (0.28) < MLM-only (1.0) = scrambled (0.99) = binary (0.99) = 9-class (1.0)"
      - "Persuasion taxonomy repositioned as interpretability tool (distinct strategy trajectories per attack type), not detection driver"
      - "Additional: zero perturbation degradation, GPT-5.5 judge F1=0.19-0.45"
    dependencies: [experiments, method, introduction]

  - section: introduction
    word_budget: 1200
    core_bullets:
      - "Hook: Multi-turn jailbreaks distribute adversarial intent across benign turns, defeating per-turn safety filters and content-level reasoning"
      - "Gap: What representation properties enable cross-attack generalization? Vanilla encoders fail catastrophically (F1=0.28 on unseen DD). LLM judges (GPT-5.5 F1=0.19-0.45) cannot detect process-level manipulation. No work systematically decomposes what makes the OOD gap bridgeable."
      - "Our angle: We hypothesized persuasion-specific classification would be key. Systematic ablation falsified this: MLM continued pretraining without any labels achieves F1=1.0 on unseen attacks. Domain exposure, not task-specific supervision, is the mechanism."
      - "Evidence hierarchy: vanilla (0.28) < MLM-only (1.0) = scrambled (0.99) = binary (0.99) = 9-class (1.0)"
      - "Contributions rewritten: (1) Systematic decomposition identifying domain exposure as mechanism, (2) MLM control as definitive causal test, (3) Persuasion taxonomy repositioned as interpretability layer"
    dependencies: [experiments]
    citation_hints:
      - "Li et al. 2024 (multi-turn human jailbreaks)"
      - "Bullwinkel et al. 2025 (representation engineering)"
      - "Gururangan et al. 2020 (DAPT)"
      - "DeepContext 2026"
    figures: [fig1_architecture]
    registry_refs: [plan_006_cross_attack_clean, plan_017_mlm_control, plan_004_perturbation_eval, plan_012_gpt55_judge]

  - section: related_work
    word_budget: 1000
    core_bullets:
      - "Multi-turn jailbreak attacks: Crescendo, FITD, DD, ActorAttack, PAIR. Persuasion is systematic (PAP, Hagendorff). Wei et al. taxonomy."
      - "Multi-turn defenses: DeepContext, BIID, G-Guard, SafeDream. All lack grounding in what makes representations transfer."
      - "Production safety systems: Llama Guard, ShieldGemma, WildGuard, Constitutional Classifiers. Per-turn, no trajectory modeling."
      - "Domain-adaptive pretraining and multi-task learning: Gururangan 2020 DAPT, Phang 2018 STILTs, Caruana 1997 MTL, Liu 2019 MT-DNN, Howard & Ruder 2018 ULMFiT. DisPeD's fine-tuning as domain-adaptive approach — MLM control confirms this framing."
      - "Discourse and persuasion: RST, PDTB, dialogue acts, Speech Act Theory, computational persuasion."
      - "Dataset artifacts and adaptive attacks."
    dependencies: [introduction]

  - section: method
    word_budget: 1400
    core_bullets:
      - "Problem formulation: binary classification over user turn prefixes"
      - "Base pipeline: DeBERTa-v3-base encoder → BiGRU aggregation → MLP classifier"
      - "Variant 1 (full model): Multi-task fine-tuning with 9-class persuasion + binary intent. L = L_strategy + 0.3 * L_intent"
      - "Variant 2 (scrambled): Same architecture, randomly permuted strategy labels"
      - "Variant 3 (binary): Collapse 9-class to persuasion-present/absent"
      - "Variant 4 (MLM): Continued pretraining with 15% masking on same training conversations, no classification heads"
      - "Variant 5 (vanilla): Unfine-tuned DeBERTa encoder (control)"
      - "Design rationale: ablation-centric structure — progressively strip components to isolate mechanism"
      - "9-class taxonomy description retained for interpretability analysis"
    dependencies: []
    figures: [fig1_architecture]

  - section: experiments
    word_budget: 2800
    core_bullets:
      - "Setup: 250 Crescendo + 250 benign (train), 80 FITD + 80 DD (OOD), 76 IID test, 76 perturbed"
      - "NEW main comparison table: Evidence hierarchy — vanilla, MLM, scrambled, binary, 9-class × K values. Core story: vanilla (0.28) < MLM (1.0) = all classification variants"
      - "Cross-attack generalization: MLM F1=1.0 on DD across all K, matching 9-class. Vanilla collapses to 0.28. TF-IDF=0.944."
      - "Ablation reframed: NOT 'which component matters' but 'systematic decomposition reveals domain exposure as mechanism'"
      - "MLM IID results: K1=0.96, K2=0.974, K3=0.987, K5=1.0, Full=1.0 (slightly below 9-class at early K, but matches at K≥5)"
      - "Perturbation robustness: Treatment 0pp, Baseline -11.8pp, TF-IDF -1.3pp"
      - "LLM judge: GPT-5.5 F1=0.19-0.45, Qwen3-8B F1=0.0-0.15"
      - "Strategy trajectory analysis: interpretability contribution, NOT detection mechanism"
      - "Honest acknowledgments: TF-IDF=0.944, MLM perturbation robustness not yet tested"
    dependencies: []
    registry_refs:
      - plan_017_mlm_control
      - plan_006_cross_attack_clean
      - plan_002_clean_training
      - plan_002_seed456
      - plan_002_seed123
      - plan_004_perturbation_eval
      - plan_013_none_collapse
      - plan_015_dd_eval
      - plan_003_scrambled_ablation
      - plan_012_gpt55_judge
      - plan_012_llm_judge_qwen
```

```json
{
  "command": "# Most human msgs are post-DDL admin (anonymization, audit). Check earlier dirs for direction-setting human msgs\npython3 << 'EOF'\nimport json, os, glob\n# Check ALL feishu chats for human user msgs\nfiles = glob.glob('/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/logs/chats/feishu_*.jsonl')\nfiles += glob.glob('/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/logs/chats/archive/feishu_*.jsonl')\nfrom collections import Counter\nday_counts = Counter()\nkey_msgs = []\nfor f in files:\n    try:\n        msgs = [json.loads(l) for l in open(f)]\n    except: continue\n    for m in msgs:\n        if m.get('role')!='user': continue\n        text = m.get('text','')\n        if text.startswith(('[飞书用户','[ralph_respond')):\n            d = m['ts'][:10]\n            day_counts[d]+=1\n            # Save key strategic messages \n            if any(k in text for k in ['都跑上','抓紧','4 项','重要','立即','urgent','紧急','停','切换','reframe']):\n                key_msgs.append((m['ts'][:19], text[:300].replace('\\n',' | ')))\n\nprint('All human msgs by day:')\nfor d in sorted(day_counts): print(f'  {d}: {day_counts[d]}')\nprint(f'\\nKey strategic human msgs ({len(key_msgs)}):')\nfor ts, t in key_msgs[:30]: print(f'\\n[{ts}] {t}')\nEOF",
  "description": "Find all human msgs across all chats"
}
```
